# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per stationary robot (penalizes stopping)
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot (penalizes moving)
- Switch time is random between 400-600 steps, run is 1200 steps
- Green wall lights up at switch time - robots can perceive this with C=7 (color perception)

**Optimal strategy:**
- Phase 1 (steps 0-400/600): ALL robots should MOVE → minimize stationary robots
- Phase 2 (steps 400/600-1200): ALL robots should STOP → minimize moving robots
- Transition trigger: perceive GREEN color (color 2) with camera → switch from moving to stopped

**Analysis of champion (score 12814.71):**
- State 0: Repulsion (moving) → transitions to Stop based on neighbor counts and floor gray
- State 1: Stop → transitions back to Repulsion based on neighbors/floor
- This doesn't use green color detection! The key insight is to use C=7 with color 2 (green) to detect the wall lighting up.

**Better design:**
- State 0: Exploration/movement (before switch)
- State 1: Stop (after switch, triggered by seeing green)
- Transition from moving→stop: perceive green (C=7, l=2)
- Transition from stop→moving: low probability (rarely go back)

The champion uses repulsion + stop with neighbor/floor conditions but misses the green light signal. Let me design machines that exploit the green signal.

**Plan:**
1. Champion (exact copy) - survival
2-5. Exploitations of champion (tweak parameters)
6-8. New designs using green color detection (C=7, l=2) as transition trigger
9-14. Exploration with different topologies

# ROUND 3/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:**
The champion uses: Attraction (moving) → Stop, triggered by seeing GREEN (color 2) with high probability. The stop state has a tiny probability of returning to attraction. This is exactly the right strategy:
- Before green light: robots move (attraction keeps them active)
- After green light: robots see it and switch to Stop

The pattern `State0=Move → Stop on seeing green` is clearly winning. The champion uses Attraction (B=4), and the 3rd best uses Exploration (B=0). Let me exploit this heavily.

**Key observations:**
1. Champion: Attraction(att=2) → Stop on green (p=0.9) or random (p=0.001)
2. Stop → Attraction with tiny p=0.001 (almost never returns)
3. Exploration also works well (score 10169)
4. The transition back from Stop should be rare/nonexistent
5. 2-state machines are best performers; 3-state machines perform poorly

**Exploitation plan:**
- Vary att gain (1.5, 2.5, 3.0, 4.0)
- Vary green detection probability (0.85, 0.95, 1.0)
- Vary return probability from Stop (0.0005, 0.002, 0.0)
- Try adding neighbor-count transitions to the moving state
- Try Repulsion as moving state (to spread out and search for green)
- Try Exploration with different rwm values

**Critical: The transition from Stop back to Move should be essentially zero.** The champion uses p=0.001 which is ~0.1% per step. Over 800 steps after switch, that's significant. Maybe even lower is better.

**Also: What if we add a second condition on the moving state?** Like transitioning to stop also when neighbors are high (clustering = good signal?). Actually no - we want to keep moving until we see green.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 3:**
- Champion: Attraction(att=2) + 3 transitions: green→stop (p=0.9), random→stop (p=0.0005), neighbors>8→stop (w=10). Stop→Attraction (p=0.0005).
- The extra transition "neighbors above 8" is the key improvement! When robots cluster (attraction brings them together), many neighbors signals "time to stop" - this is a proxy for being near the green wall area.
- Very low return probability (0.0005) is better than 0.001 or 0.0001
- Higher attraction gain (3.0, 4.0) is worse than 2.0 for the 3-transition version
- The "neighbors below threshold" transition (C=4) made things worse

**Why does neighbors>8 help?**
When robots cluster near the green wall (attracted to each other), they have many neighbors. This gives an additional signal to stop. The threshold of 8 with steepness 10 is quite sharp.

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Try different neighbor thresholds (6, 7, 9, 10) with similar steepness
2. Try different steepness values (5, 15, 20) for the neighbor condition
3. Try different green detection probabilities (0.95, 1.0)
4. Try adding a 4th transition (e.g., floor gray with some probability)
5. Try att=2 with slightly different random return probability (0.0003, 0.0008)
6. Try att=1.5 with the 3-transition structure

**Exploration ideas:**
- What if Stop state also has a green-detection transition back to moving (to handle false stops before green)?
- Try repulsion + same trigger structure (spread out, then stop on green/cluster)

- Very high steepness (20) is bad when combined with extra stop transitions
- Repulsion is much worse than attraction

**Critical insight: lower return probability is better.** 0.0003 < 0.0005. Maybe even lower (0.0001 was tried before at score 10998 without the neighbor condition - let me try 0.0001 WITH the neighbor condition).

**What to try:**
1. Vary return probability: 0.0001, 0.0002, 0.00015, 0.00005 (with neighbor condition)
2. Vary neighbor threshold: 6, 7, 9, 10 (keeping w=10, p_return=0.0003)
3. Vary steepness: 12, 18, 20 (keeping threshold=8, p_return=0.0003)
4. Try 4-transition version: add another stop trigger
5. Try green detection p=0.95 or 1.0 with lower return prob
6. Vary att gain slightly: 1.5, 2.5 with best params

**What not to do:**
- Don't add extra transitions to the Stop state
- Don't use repulsion
- Don't use exploration

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 5:**
- Champion holds at 6581.12 (p_return=0.0003)
- p=0.0001 scored 6595 - very close to champion, slightly worse
- p=0.0002 scored 6834
- The trend: 0.0001 ≈ 0.0003 < 0.0002 < 0.0005 (non-monotonic, noisy)
- att=1.5 scored 6891 (slightly worse than att=2)
- p_green=0.95 scored 7044 (worse than 0.9)
- threshold=10 (w=10) scored 10850 - TERRIBLE! threshold=8 is key
- threshold=9 with p=0.0003 wasn't shown but threshold=8 clearly optimal

**What parameters matter most:**
1. Neighbor threshold: 8 is optimal (10 was terrible)
2. Return probability: ~0.0003 or 0.0001 seem best
3. att gain: 2.0 seems best
4. Green detection: 0.9 seems best
5. Steepness: 10-12 seems good

**New exploration ideas:**
- Try p_return = 0 (no return transition at all from Stop) - but this requires n1=0... actually minimum is n=1. Try p=0.00001 (essentially 0)
- Try threshold=8, w=10 but with p_return varying more finely: 0.00015, 0.00025, 0.00035
- Try adding a 4th transition to moving state: e.g., also stop if neighbors below 1 (isolated robot should stop?)... wait no - isolated robot should keep moving to find others
- Try different neighbor thresholds: 6, 7 (lower than 8)
- Try att=2 with threshold=8, w=10, p=0.0001 (best combo not yet tried together?)
- Actually: p=0.0001 WITH w=10, threshold=8 scored 6595. Champion uses p=0.0003 with same. Let me try finer grid around 0.0001-0.0003.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 7:**
- New champion: p_return=0 (zero!), threshold=8, w=8, att=2, green=0.9 → score 6090.95
- The champion has p=0 for both random transitions - robots NEVER return from Stop!
- This makes perfect sense: once stopped (after seeing green or clustering), stay stopped forever
- att=2.5 with p=0.0001 also scored 6302 (same as att=2 with same params)
- green=0.85 also competitive at 6306

**Critical insight: p=0 is optimal for the return transition.** The stop state is truly absorbing. This means the only way back would be another condition, but we don't want robots to go back at all.

**What to explore now:**
1. Vary threshold (6, 7, 9) with p=0, w=8 - can we stop even earlier/later?
2. Vary w (4, 6, 10, 12, 20) with p=0, threshold=8 - steepness effects
3. Vary green detection prob (0.8, 0.85, 0.95, 1.0) with p=0
4. Vary att gain (1.5, 2.5, 3.0) with p=0, threshold=8, w=8
5. Try 4 transitions: add another condition to trigger stop (e.g., also stop on floor gray with some prob, or another neighbor threshold)
6. Try making Stop state have n=0 transitions... but minimum is 1. With p=0, effectively absorbing.
7. Try different combination: what if neighbors>8 uses w=8 but threshold=7 or 6?

**Also: the Stop state still needs a transition declared but p=0 makes it never fire. That's our current champion structure.**

**Important: threshold=6 scored 9341 (terrible). The neighbor condition fires too early when threshold is low, causing robots to stop before the green light.**

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 9:**
- Champion holds at 5968.93 (att=2, green=0.9, p=0, threshold=8, w=12)
- att=1.5 scored 6290 (better than att=2 in this round, but champion still leads)
- 2-transition (green + neighbor only, no random) scored 6411 - decent!
- threshold=7 scored 8168 (bad), threshold=9 scored 7033 (mediocre), threshold=8 is optimal
- w=15 also good (6479), w=12 and w=15 essentially tied

**Final round strategy - maximize exploitation near champion:**
1. The champion structure is very stable: att=2, green=0.9, p_random=0, threshold=8, w=12
2. Try att values around 1.5-2.5 more finely: 1.0, 1.2, 1.7, 2.0, 2.2
3. Try w values around 12-15: 13, 14, 16
4. Try green prob: 0.9 is best, but maybe 0.92 or 0.88?
5. The 2-transition version (no random, just green+neighbor) is interesting - try with different params
6. Try att=1.5 with w variations around 12

**Key observations:**
- threshold=8 is definitively optimal (7 and 9 both worse)
- p_return=0 is optimal
- att=2 is champion but att=1.5 performed well too
- w=12-15 seems best range

**Last round - go deep on exploitation:**

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 22 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.52 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
